Contains the strict CSP **with `frame-ancestors`**. - **`scripts/generate-headers.mjs`** — postbuild step. Reads Astro's per-page `sha256` hashes from the build, merges them into `dist/_headers` using `public/_headers` as the base (so `frame-ancestors` survives). - **`astro.config.mjs`** — `security.csp` enabled. Note the Astro 7 schema constraint: `script-src`/`style-src` cannot go in `directives`; they must use `scriptDirective`/`styleDirective`. `frame-ancestors` removed (it's ignored in `<meta>`, and `_headers` sends it as a real header). - Final CSP allows `data:` in `connect-src` and `'unsafe-eval'` in `script-src`: - `data:` because the worker test does `fetch('data:image/png;base64,...')`. - `'unsafe-eval'` because the libheif WASM decoder requires it. This is why HEIC was broken. - **`scripts/apply-pages-base-path.mjs`** — had `process.exit(0)` for root deploys, which prevented the header generator from running. Fixed; log messages scrubbed of "GitHub Pages". - **`scripts/verify-pages-output.mjs`** — verifier. Rejects the placeholder domain `https://velocedrop.example`, enforces title ≤70 chars. Reverted repeatedly to avoid noise. **A deterministic `prebuild` would fix this permanently.** - **Contact page:** `/id/info/kontak/` and `/en/info/contact/` — real copy, email as `mailto:` link, copy button reads from `data-email` instead of a hardcoded `'hello@velocedrop.example'`, link underlined + monospace (was color-only, WCAG 1.4.1 violation). ## Decisions and constraints - **Refused the 6000-word × 30 target.** Delivered ~700–1000 words × 30 instead. Rationale given to the user: 180,000 words in one session means invented padding, which is exactly Google's *scaled content abuse* and would get the site rejected, contradicting the AdSense rule the user quoted. Long word count is not a quality signal. **Do not relitigate this** — the user accepted it with "gas lanjut samapai selesai". - **No Unsplash, no hotlinking.** `unsplash.com/napi/search` → 401, `source.unsplash.com` → 503 (shut down). `images.unsplash.com` works but there's no way to search for topically appropriate photos. Used **Openverse** with `license_type=commercial`, filtered to CC0 / CC BY / CC BY-SA / Public Domain Mark. Also rejected candidates that were NC-licensed (unusable on an ad-funded site) or contained real people's identity documents. - **Images are self-hosted**, not remote. CSP is `img-src 'self' blob: data:`, and hotlinking would void the "no third parties" privacy claim that is the product's entire differentiator. - **Automated image search is unreliable** — first pass returned buildings, a chimney, and an irrelevant postage stamp. The working method was: fetch candidates → build a **contact sheet** PNG → inspect visually → pick → download as WebP. Repeat for weak topics. - **English is default.** `/` → `/en/` (forced redirect), `x-default` → English. Tests and verifier were updated accordingly. - **Migrating English slugs required unifying routes first.** Because routing was file-based, changing slugs would have left dead links *inside* the workspaces. The fix was the catch-all route + `toolHref()` everywhere, then slugs, then 301s. - **Adsterra verdict:** technically possible, economically not worth it yet. CSP would block their scripts until `script-src`/`connect-src`/`frame-src` add their domains; UK traffic requires a real CMP for GDPR/PECR; popunders contradict the privacy positioning; realistic CPM gives ~$6/month at 10k pageviews. Recommended order: deploy → Search Console → content → monetize last, and ads only on guide pages, never in the tool workspace. - **`'unsafe-eval'` is now in the CSP** because of the HEIC WASM decoder — this weakens any "we don't run eval" security argument and should be mentioned before adding any third-party ad network. - **Force-push consequence:** after the history rewrite, any other clone must run `git fetch && git reset --hard origin/master`, never `git pull`. ## Errors and feedback - **Mojibake false alarm, three times.** `Â·`, `â€œ`, `Â© 2026` all appeared in PowerShell output. Verified with Node and `git ls-files --eol` that source files are clean UTF-8; it is purely a console-decoding artifact. **Always verify encoding with Node, not PowerShell.** - **`AdSlot` grep false negative.** An early grep concluded the component was unused; it is actually imported on the homepage. Don't trust a single grep on this repo. - **CSP killed the whole site on Netlify.** Proven by serving `dist/` with the real `_headers`: `catalogScriptRan: false`, `mobileNavInitialized: false`, 4 violations. Root cause: 179 inline `<script>` tags (3 executable — Astro router, island hydration, mobile nav) with `script-src 'self'`. `astro preview` doesn't read `_headers`, which is why it looked fine locally. Fixed via Astro 7 hash-based CSP + header generator. - **HEIC broken by CSP.** Two sequential blockers: `connect-src` missing `data:`, then the WASM decoder needing `'unsafe-eval'`. `security-headers.test.mjs` explicitly forbade `unsafe-eval` and had to be updated with documented reasoning. - **Two CSPs conflict.** `netlify.toml` originally declared a hash-less CSP that would combine with `_headers`. Removed entirely from TOML. - **`git filter-branch` quoting/path failures.** Backslashes split by the shell; `filter-branch` changes cwd so relative script paths break. Fixed with an absolute path using forward slashes and a script placed inside the repo. The bot trailer was on the **root commit**, outside the range initially targeted — had to rewrite from HEAD. - **`update-ref --stdin` failed** due to Windows line endings; deleted `refs/original` entries one at a time. - **Astro content collection failures (several, mostly self-inflicted):** probe files missing the closing `---` (whole file parsed as template); `getCollection` unresolved until `astro sync` ran (data store was empty); `_`-prefixed files silently ignored by Astro; article IDs colliding across languages so `.id.md` overwrote `.en.md` — fixed with `generateId` preserving a language suffix while URLs stay clean. - **YAML/schema errors:** unquoted colon in an article title; `summary` fields over the schema length limit (one by a single character); four Indonesian titles exceeding 70 chars — fixed by dropping the `| VeloceDrop` suffix **for article pages only**. - **Accessibility bugs caught by axe (both would have shipped):** - `link-in-text-block` — "Guides" link in article meta was color-only. Added underline. - `aria-pressed="false"` landing on `<li>` article cards, because the selector `[data-category]` matched cards *and* filter buttons. Scoped to `button[data-category]`. - **Interaction audit typed 256 into the RGB field and expected it stored.** Clamping to 255 is correct behaviour; the audit was wrong. Constrained the audit to respect `max`. - **Playwright WebKit never lazy-loads images from `scrollIntoViewIfNeeded`** — only real scrolling triggers it. Fixed the test with `window.scrollTo`, not the app. Real Safari users scroll normally. - **Manual dev server on port 4321** caused 404 tests to fail across all three engines because Playwright reused it. Kill the server before running suites. - **Verify exit code / node server workaround:** `serve` wasn't installed; used a plain Node static server instead (proven to work in an earlier session — reuse it). - **Test brittleness found repeatedly:** hardcoded counts (schema blocks 3→4, landing sections 5→6, "documents" count 2→3) and over-specific assertions ("passport" legitimately matching 2 articles). 10: export default function Compressor({ lang, initialTarget = 200 }: { lang: string; initialTarget?: number }) {
11:   const en = lang === 'en';
12:   const [file, setFile] = useState<File | null>(null);
13:   const [sourceUrl, setSourceUrl] = useState('');
14:   const [result, setResult] = useState<Result | null>(null);

16:   const [format, setFormat] = useState('image/jpeg');

28:     if (incoming) { choose(incoming); return; }
29:     if (new URLSearchParams(location.search).get('example') !== 'workspace') return;
30:     let cancelled = false;
31:     void (async () => {
32:       try {
33:         const response = await fetch('/images/landing-workspace-sample.webp', { credentials: 'same-origin' });
34:         if (!response.ok) throw new Error('sample-unavailable');
35:         const blob = await response.blob();
36:         if (blob.type !== 'image/webp' || blob.size > limit) throw new Error('sample-invalid');
37:         if (!cancelled) choose(new File([blob], 'velocedrop.webp', { type: blob.type }));
38:       } catch {
39:         if (!cancelled) setError(en ? 'The example could not be loaded. Choose an image to continue.' : 'Foto contoh tidak dapat dimuat. Pilih gambar untuk melanjutkan.');
40:       }
41:     })();
42:     return () => { cancelled = true; };
43:   }, []);

45:   function clearResult() { setResult(null); setSplit(50); setZoom(1); setPan({ x: 0, y: 0 }); }
46:   function choose(next?: File) {
47:     clearResult(); setError(''); setFile(next ?? null);
48:     setSourceUrl(previous => { if (previous) URL.revokeObjectURL(previous); return next ? URL.createObjectURL(next) : ''; });
49:   }
50: 
51:   async function process(allowResize = false) {
52:     if (!file || busy) return;
53:     setError(''); clearResult(); setBusy(true);
54:     let bitmap: ImageBitmap | undefined;
55:     try {
56:       if (file.size > limit) throw new Error(en ? 'The file exceeds 20 MB.' : 'Berkas melebihi 20 MB.');
57:       if (!Number.isFinite(target) || target < 1 || target > 20000) throw new Error(en ? 'Enter a target between 1 and 20,000 KB.' : 'Masukkan target antara 1 dan 20.000 KB.');
58:       const header = readImageHeader(new Uint8Array(await file.slice(0, 1_048_576).arrayBuffer()));
59:       if (!header) throw new Error(en ? 'Choose a valid JPEG, PNG or WebP image.' : 'Pilih gambar JPEG, PNG, atau WebP yang valid.');
60:       if (header.width * header.height > 40_000_000) throw new Error(en ? 'Image exceeds the 40 megapixel limit.' : 'Gambar melebihi batas 40 megapiksel.');
61:       bitmap = await createImageBitmap(file, { imageOrientation: 'from-image' });
62:       if (bitmap.width * bitmap.height > 40_000_000) throw new Error(en ? 'Image exceeds the 40 megapixel limit.' : 'Gambar melebihi batas 40 megapiksel.');
63: 
64:       let best: { blob: Blob; width: number; height: number; met: boolean } | undefined;
65:       const attempts = allowResize ? 10 : 1;
66:       for (let attempt = 0; attempt < attempts; attempt++) {
67:         const scale = allowResize ? Math.pow(0.85, attempt) : 1;
68:         const width = Math.max(1, Math.round(bitmap.width * scale));
69:         const height = Math.max(1, Math.round(bitmap.height * scale));
70:         if (allowResize && (width < 96 || height < 96)) break;
71:         const canvas = document.createElement('canvas'); canvas.width = width; canvas.height = height;
72:         const ctx = canvas.getContext('2d'); if (!ctx) throw new Error('Canvas unavailable');
73:         if (format === 'image/jpeg') { ctx.fillStyle = '#ffffff'; ctx.fillRect(0, 0, width, height); }
74:         ctx.drawImage(bitmap, 0, 0, width, height);
75:         const encode = (quality: number) => new Promise<Blob>((resolve, reject) => canvas.toBlob(blob => blob && blob.type === format ? resolve(blob) : reject(new Error(en ? 'This browser cannot encode the selected format.' : 'Browser ini tidak dapat membuat format yang dipilih.')), format, quality));
76:         let smallest: Blob | undefined;
77:         let meeting: Blob | undefined;
78:         const qualities = [0.1, 0.25, 0.4, 0.55, 0.7, 0.85, 1];
79:         for (const quality of qualities) {
80:           const blob = await encode(quality);
81:           if (!smallest || blob.size < smallest.size) smallest = blob;
82:           if (blob.size <= target * 1000 && (!meeting || blob.size > meeting.size)) meeting = blob;
83:         }
84:         const candidate = meeting ?? smallest!;
85:         if (meeting) { best = { blob: candidate, width, height, met: true }; break; }
86:         if (!best || candidate.size < best.blob.size) best = { blob: candidate, width, height, met: false };
87:         canvas.width = 0; canvas.height = 0;
88:       }
89:       if (!best) throw new Error(en ? 'No usable output could be created.' : 'Tidak ada hasil yang dapat dibuat.');
90:       setResult({ url: URL.createObjectURL(best.blob), blob: best.blob, size: best.blob.size, width: best.width, height: best.height, type: format, met: best.met, resized: best.width !== bitmap.width || best.height !== bitmap.height });
91:     } catch (err) { setError(err instanceof Error ? err.message : (en ? 'Could not read this image.' : 'Gambar tidak dapat dibaca.')); }
92:     finally { bitmap?.close(); setBusy(false); }
93:   }

import { catalog, type Locale } from '../../tool-registry';

type ToolProps = {
  kind: 'tool';
  lang: Locale;
  toolId: string;
  title: string;
  lead: string;
  description: string;
  preset?: string;
  targetKB?: number;

type PrivacyProps = {
  kind: 'privacy';
  lang: Locale;
  title: string;
  description: string;

export function getStaticPaths() {
  const toolLead: Record<string, { id: string; en: string }> = {
    'compress-image': { id: 'Pilih gambar, tentukan target, lalu unduh hasilnya.', en: 'Choose an image, set a target, and download the actual result.' },
    batch: { id: 'Tambahkan gambar, tentukan target, lalu unduh hasil satu per satu atau sebagai ZIP.', en: 'Add images, set a target and download each result or a ZIP.' },
    'resize-image': { id: 'Atur dimensi hasil, pilih mode crop, lalu unduh PNG.', en: 'Set output dimensions, choose a crop mode, and download a PNG.' },
    'image-converter': { id: 'Pilih format sumber, periksa pratinjau hasil, lalu unduh berkas konversi.', en: 'Choose a source format, inspect the output preview, and download the converted file.' },
    background: { id: 'Muat model, pilih potret, lalu perbaiki mask.', en: 'Load the model, choose a portrait and refine the mask.' },
    'logo-watermark': { id: 'Tambahkan logo pada beberapa gambar produk, lalu unduh hasilnya.', en: 'Place a logo on multiple product images and download the results.' },
    'photo-pdf': { id: 'Susun gambar, pilih ukuran kertas, lalu buat PDF.', en: 'Arrange your images, choose paper size, then create a PDF.' },
    'pdf-jpg': { id: 'Pilih halaman dan resolusi, lalu unduh gambar.', en: 'Select pages and resolution, then download the images.' },
    'pdf-500kb': { id: 'Pilih target, baca penjelasan rasterisasi, lalu buat PDF.', en: 'Choose a target, review the rasterization warning, then create a PDF.' },
    print: { id: 'Pilih foto dan buat PDF sesuai ukuran cetak.', en: 'Choose a photo and create a print-sized PDF.' },
  };

  const privacyCopy = {
    id: {
      title: 'Privasi',
      description: 'Pelajari cara berkas diproses di browser, disimpan sementara dalam memori sesi, dan aset situs yang tetap memerlukan jaringan.',
    },
    en: {
      title: 'Privacy',
      description: 'Learn how files are processed in your browser, kept temporarily in session memory, and which site assets still use a network connection.',
    },
  } as const;

  const paths: Array<{ params: { lang: string; path: string }; props: ToolProps | PrivacyProps }> = [];

  for (const tool of catalog) {
    for (const lang of ['id', 'en'] as const) {
      paths.push({
        params: { lang, path: tool.path[lang].replace(/^\/+|\/+$/g, '').replace(new RegExp(`^${lang}/`), '') },
        props: {
          kind: 'tool',
          lang,
          toolId: tool.id,
          title: tool.name[lang],
          lead: toolLead[tool.id][lang],
          description: tool.seo?.description[lang] ?? tool.description[lang],
          preset: tool.presetKey,
          targetKB: tool.targetKB,
        },
      });
    }
  }

  for (const lang of ['id', 'en'] as const) {
    paths.push({
      params: { lang, path: lang === 'id' ? 'privasi' : 'privacy' },
      props: { kind: 'privacy', lang, title: privacyCopy[lang].title, description: privacyCopy[lang].description },
    });
  }

  return paths;
}

You rely on the operator's word for what happens.

.\src\content\articles\why-200kb-targets-fail.en.md:46:If your 200 KB target is a hard requirement — a job portal that rejects larger files, a form with a strict limit — switching the output format to WebP is often the single most effective step. .\src\content\articles\jpeg-vs-webp-for-web-uploads.en.md:24:Use **WebP** when you control where the image goes — your own site, a modern marketplace, a social platform. It produces a smaller file at the same visual quality.

.\src\content\articles\image-formats-explained.en.md:30:The classic mistake is screenshotting code, saving as JPEG, and wondering why the text is fuzzy. JPEG's block-based compression spends its precision on gradients and destroys high-contrast edges — exactly the opposite of what text needs.
.\src\content\articles\image-formats-explained.en.md:32:## PNG — lossless, with transparency

.\src\content\articles\image-formats-explained.en.md:52:## HEIC — what iPhones produce
.\src\content\articles\image-formats-explained.en.md:56:**Use for:** nothing by choice — it is what the camera produced.
.\src\content\articles\image-formats-explained.en.md:62:## SVG — vector, not raster
.\src\content\articles\image-formats-explained.en.md:70:SVG can contain scripts, external references, and embedded HTML, which makes it a security concern. Sanitization before rasterizing is not optional — a malicious SVG can execute script in some contexts. VeloceDrop rejects SVG files containing scripts or external resources rather than trying to strip them.
.\src\content\articles\image-formats-explained.en.md:72:## ICO — Windows icons

.\src\content\articles\compress-image-without-losing-quality.en.md:44:For screenshots, diagrams, logos, and anything with text or sharp geometry, PNG is usually the right answer and lossy compression is the wrong tool entirely. Let me plan the tool: Document Scanner.

Requirements:
- Camera-based scanning like Google Drive scanner
- Auto-detect document when pointed at it
- Result like Google Drive scan
- Friendly UX
- Output image or PDF
- Correct logic
- Consistent design system

Implementation plan:
- `src/components/DocumentScanner.tsx` — React island
- getUserMedia with `facingMode: environment`
- Live frame analysis: downscale frame to small canvas, run edge detection (Sobel) + find largest quadrilateral (document contour). Use a simple algorithm: grayscale → blur → Sobel gradient magnitude → threshold → find contours... That's heavy. Simpler robust approach: 
  - Compute grayscale at low res (e.g., 160px wide)
  - Gradient magnitude
  - Binary mask with adaptive threshold
  - Find document boundary by scanning: for each row, find leftmost/rightmost strong edge; fit quadrilateral to the largest connected component's extreme points.
  
  A practical, dependable approach used in web scanners: detect the largest bright quadrilateral against darker background. But that fails on white docs on white table.
  
  Better: use corner detection on the gradient image + convex hull + approximate quad. Let me implement:
  1. Downscale to max 256 wide.
  2. Grayscale.
  3. Gaussian blur 5x5.
  4. Sobel → magnitude.
  5. Otsu threshold on magnitude.
  6. Morphological dilation to connect edges.
  7. Find connected components with area > 5% of frame.
  8. For largest component, compute convex hull, then approximate to 4 points using the rotating-calipers / "max area quadrilateral" heuristic.
  
  Simpler and more stable: instead of connected components, collect all edge pixels of large components, compute convex hull, then find the 4 hull points that maximize quadrilateral area (brute force over hull points is O(n^4) but hull is small after sampling). That's actually a known approach and quite reliable.

  Then the quad is the document. Perspective transform via canvas math (compute homography from 4 src points to 4 dst points, then per-pixel sample). Doing per-pixel in JS for a 3000x4000 image is too slow. Better: use WebGL? Or do the transform by drawing triangles with canvas transform (affine per triangle, 2 triangles) — approximate but produces seams. Actually a cleaner approach: compute homography, then render by inverse-mapping in a loop at output resolution but in chunks; for a 2000px output that's ~4M pixels — JS can do that in ~200-400ms with typed arrays. Acceptable and correct.

  Actually better and much faster: use canvas 2D with `setTransform` on triangles? Seams. Let me do the inverse mapping with typed arrays but limit output size sensibly (e.g., max 2200px longest side). That's fine.

  Hmm, but performance: 2200x3000 = 6.6M pixels; per pixel we do a few multiplies + bilinear sample (4 reads). Maybe 300-600ms. Acceptable for a scan action. Do it once on capture, not per frame. Good.

- Live overlay: draw detected quad on a canvas over the video.
- Auto-capture: when quad is stable (low variance of corner positions across ~8 frames) and area large enough, trigger capture automatically. Google Drive does auto-capture with a countdown. I'll implement: stable for ~1.2s → capture. Plus manual shutter button. Plus a "auto-capture" toggle.
- Enhancement: Google Drive scanner applies contrast/whitening. Implement "enhance" filter: grayscale-ish document enhancement — convert to luminance, apply local contrast / levels, whiten background, darken text. Provide toggle Original / Enhanced (B&W-ish) / Grayscale. Keep it honest: document filter that whitens background.
- Multi-page: capture several pages, reorder, delete, then export all as image(s) or a single PDF.
- Output: PDF via existing pdf library used by PhotoPdfStudio. Let me check what's used there (pdf-lib probably).
- File input fallback: also allow picking an existing photo to scan (important for desktop, no camera).
- Tests: unit test for the geometry math (quad detection + homography), and a Playwright test using a synthetic image via file input.

13: async function bitmapFromFile(file:File):Promise<ImageBitmap>{
14:   const header=await readImageBlobHeader(file);
15:   if(!header)throw new Error('format');if(header.width*header.height>MAX_PIXELS)throw new Error('pixels');
16:   const bitmap=await createImageBitmap(file,{imageOrientation:'from-image'});
17:   if(bitmap.width*bitmap.height>MAX_PIXELS){bitmap.close();throw new Error('pixels')}
18:   return bitmap;
19: }
20: async function toBlob(canvas:HTMLCanvasElement):Promise<Blob>{return new Promise((resolve,reject)=>canvas.toBlob(value=>value?.type==='image/png'?resolve(value):reject(new Error('encode')),'image/png'))}

32:   async function addFiles(selected:File[]){

34:     setBusy(true);setErrors([]);invalidate();const added:Item[]=[];const problems:string[]=[];
35:     let total=items.reduce((sum,item)=>sum+item.file.size,0);

40:         let bitmap:ImageBitmap|undefined;

45:           const thumbnail=await toBlob(canvas);if(!alive.current)break;const url=URL.createObjectURL(thumbnail);previews.current.add(url);
46:           added.push({id:++nextId.current,file,preview:url,width:bitmap.width,height:bitmap.height,rotation:0});total+=file.size;
47:         }catch{problems.push(`${file.name}: ${en?'unsupported, corrupt, or exceeds 20 megapixels':'format tidak didukung, rusak, atau melebihi 20 megapiksel'}`)}finally{bitmap?.close()}
48:       }

53:   function move(index:number,offset:number){const target=index+offset;if(target<0||target>=items.length)return;const next=[...items];[next[index],next[target]]=[next[target],next[index]];setItems(next);invalidate()}
54:   function reorder(from:number,to:number){if(from===to||from<0||to<0)return;const next=[...items];const [item]=next.splice(from,1);next.splice(to,0,item);setItems(next);invalidate()}
55:   async function create(){
56:     if(!items.length||busy)return;setBusy(true);setProgress(0);setErrors([]);invalidate();
57:     try{

70:           const scale=Math.min((pageWidth-2*padding)/canvas.width,(pageHeight-2*padding)/canvas.height);
71:           const width=canvas.width*scale,height=canvas.height*scale;const page=pdf.addPage([pageWidth,pageHeight]);

2. **Add 11th tool: document scanner** — camera capture, automatic document detection, perspective correction, output PNG/JPG or PDF.

This is the big piece. Requirements:
- Works like Google Drive scanner: camera preview, auto-detect document edges while aiming, capture, crop/perspective correct, enhance, output image or PDF.
- "ramah pengguna" UX with existing design system.
- Correct logic.

Technical approach without new heavy deps:
- `getUserMedia` for camera with `environment` facing mode.
- Document edge detection: grayscale → downsample → Sobel/edge magnitude → find largest quadrilateral. Implementing a full contour finder is heavy. A pragmatic and *correct* approach: 
  - Downscale frame to ~320px wide.
  - Compute grayscale + gradient magnitude (Sobel).
  - Instead of full contour tracing, detect document as the largest bright quad: use a coarse approach — compute row/column projection? 
  
  Honestly, a robust contour + convex hull + quadrilateral approximation is ~200 lines. Let me think about what is genuinely implementable and correct.

Simpler robust approach used by many JS scanners: adaptive threshold + connected components / contour following, then `cv.approxPolyDP`-like Douglas-Peucker simplification to 4 points.

I can implement:
1. Grayscale + box blur.
2. Sobel magnitude.
3. Binary threshold (Otsu).
4. Morphological close to connect edges.
5. Find largest connected component of "edge" pixels near border... hmm, edges are thin lines, not filled.

Alternative that's much more reliable: **find the document as the largest bright region**. Documents are usually bright paper on darker background. But that fails on dark backgrounds/wood.

Better robust approach: Implement contour tracing on the thresholded edge map, compute convex hull per contour, then approximate hull to 4 vertices and score by area + convexity. That's the classic pipeline and it's implementable.

Practical simplification that still yields good UX: 
- Use gradient-based edge map, then find extreme points: for each of 8 directions (or 4 quadrants) find the extreme edge pixel → gives a quad from the outer boundary of edge pixels. Actually a well-known trick: take all edge pixels, compute for 4 quadrant sums the point maximizing (x+y), (x−y), etc. That gives corners robustly for a document filling much of the frame. Then verify it's a plausible convex quad with min area.

Hmm, that's honestly what many simple scanners do and it works decently. But it fails when background clutter has strong edges outside the document.

Let me do a hybrid, and be honest in code/docs about heuristics:
1. Downscale to width 240.
2. Grayscale, blur, Sobel → edge magnitude.
3. Threshold at a high percentile (e.g., 97th) to keep only strong edges.
4. Contour trace (Moore neighborhood) on binary map, collect contours with length > threshold.
5. For each contour: convex hull (Andrew monotone chain), then if hull has >4 points, reduce to 4 by Douglas–Peucker on the closed hull; if that yields 4 points, candidate.
6. Score = area * rectangularity (area / minAreaRect area) → pick best with rectangularity > 0.7 and area > 15% of frame.
7. Return the quad in full-resolution coordinates.

That's genuinely correct-ish and testable. I can unit-test the geometry functions (quad ordering, perspective transform math, area) without a camera.

Also need: 
- **Perspective correction**: compute homography from quad → target rectangle, then render via canvas. Canvas 2D can't do arbitrary perspective. Options: (a) software warp on ImageData (slow but fine for downscaled docs, ~2000px), or (b) WebGL. Simplest correct: implement inverse mapping with bilinear sampling in JS on ImageData. For a 2000×2800 output that's 5.6M pixels × few ops — acceptable (a second or so). I'll downscale camera capture to max ~2400px.

Actually better: do the homography with a WebGL shader? Too much. Software warp is fine and deterministic. I'll implement `warpPerspective` with bilinear interpolation.

- **Enhancement**: grayscale + contrast stretch / "magic color" like Drive. Implement: adaptive-ish white balance + CLAHE-lite? Simpler: per-channel percentile stretch + unsharp. I'll offer modes: Original, Grayscale (document), Enhanced (contrast+white point), B&W (threshold). Keep math honest.

- **Multi-page**: capture multiple pages, then export as multi-page PDF (pdf-lib already a dep) or individual images.

- **Fallback for no camera**: file input so users on desktop can still use it (and tests can). Also allow drag-drop.

- **Output**: PNG/JPG download per page, or PDF with all pages.

UX: mirror remove.bg pattern — compact title, two-column, controls at top, capped preview.

Also need to handle `getUserMedia` requires secure context; on localhost fine, on Netlify https fine. Also handle permission denied gracefully with a clear message + file fallback.

2. Add 11th tool: document scanner, like Google Drive scanner. Camera-based, auto edge detection, perspective correction (homography), output image or PDF, no upload.

This is a big piece. Key technical parts:
- Camera access via getUserMedia.
- Document edge detection: downsample to grayscale, compute gradients, find largest quadrilateral. A robust implementation without OpenCV is feasible: use a gradient-based approach + Hough-ish or contour approach.
- Perspective correction: compute homography from 4 corners → rectangle, then warp. Can do with canvas per-pixel sampling (inverse mapping) — doable but slow for large images. Better: implement homography and inverse-map into output canvas at a capped resolution (e.g., max 2400px long side). Per-pixel with Uint32Array is fine.
- Auto-capture when steady/stable.
- Output: PNG/JPEG or PDF via pdf-lib.

Let me think about what's feasible. Document scanner with camera:
- getUserMedia to access camera
- Auto edge detection of document in each frame (using canvas + image processing — grayscale, blur, edge detection, then find largest quadrilateral contour)
- Capture → perspective transform (warp) to rectify document
- Multi-page capture
- Output as JPG/PNG or PDF via pdf-lib
- Also allow file upload as fallback (important for tests & non-camera devices)

Implementing contour detection without OpenCV is work but doable:
- Downscale to e.g. 320px wide
- Grayscale
- Compute gradient magnitude (Sobel) 
- Binary threshold
- Then find document quad... Actually a simpler robust approach: since a document is typically a bright rectangle on darker background, we can threshold brightness and find the largest bright connected region, then compute its corners.

Hmm, but "auto detect document" like Google Drive scanner. Google Drive scanner does edge detection with a real CV library. I need something that works reasonably.

A practical approach that's honest and robust:
1. Downscale frame to ~240px.
2. Grayscale, blur.
3. Compute Sobel gradient magnitude, threshold at high percentile → edge map.
4. Find the largest connected component? That's edge map, not filled.
5. Alternative: use "brightness + saturation" segmentation: document paper is usually bright and low saturation. Compute mask of bright pixels (luminance > Otsu threshold), then find largest connected component, compute convex hull, then fit quadrilateral via rotating calipers / min-area rect.

That's fairly robust for paper on a desk. Then refine corners with the min-area rect. Actually min-area rectangle gives a rect but perspective distortion means the document is a quadrilateral, not rectangle. But min-area rect on the convex hull of the mask approximates well for modest perspective.

Better: from largest connected bright region, get convex hull, then find the 4 extreme points: those maximizing/minimizing (x+y) and (x-y) — classic corner detection for quadrilaterals. That gives 4 corners for a rotated quad. Good approximation.

Then perspective transform: compute homography from 4 source corners to destination rect with aspect ratio estimated from edge lengths. Implement homography solve (8x8 linear system) — doable with Gaussian elimination.

That's a real implementation. Let me write it carefully.

Read 1/1 file, 40 lines
1: export type SessionFile = { id: string; blob: Blob; name: string; width?: number; height?: number; created: number };
2: const MAX_FILES = 5;
3: const MAX_BYTES = 50_000_000;
4: const TTL = 30 * 60 * 1000;
5: type State = { files: SessionFile[]; pending: { id: string; path: string } | null; listeners: Set<() => void>; timer: ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | null };
6: declare global { interface Window { __veloceSession?: State } }
7: function state(): State {
8:   return window.__veloceSession ??= {files: [], pending: null, listeners: new Set(), timer: null};
9: }
10: function emit(s: State) { for (const listener of s.listeners) listener(); }
11: function schedule(s: State) {
12:   if (s.timer) clearTimeout(s.timer);
13:   s.timer = s.files.length ? setTimeout(() => { prune(); }, Math.max(0, s.files[0].created + TTL - Date.now())) : null;
14: }
15: function prune() {
16:   const s = state(); const before = s.files.length;
17:   s.files = s.files.filter(file => file.created + TTL > Date.now());
18:   if (s.pending && !s.files.some(file => file.id === s.pending!.id)) s.pending = null;
19:   schedule(s); if (before !== s.files.length) emit(s);
20: }
21: export function listSessionFiles(): SessionFile[] { prune(); return state().files.slice().reverse(); }
22: export function subscribeSession(listener: () => void) { const s = state(); s.listeners.add(listener); return () => { s.listeners.delete(listener); }; }
23: export function addSessionFile(blob: Blob, width?: number, height?: number): string | null {
24:   if (!blob.size || blob.size > MAX_BYTES) return null;
25:   prune(); const s = state(); const id = crypto.randomUUID();
26:   const extension: Record<string,string> = {'image/png':'png','image/jpeg':'jpg','image/webp':'webp','application/pdf':'pdf','application/zip':'zip'};
27:   const name = `velocedrop.${extension[blob.type] ?? 'bin'}`;
28:   s.files.push({id, blob, name, width, height, created: Date.now()});
29:   while (s.files.length > MAX_FILES || s.files.reduce ( (sum, item) => sum + item.blob.size, 0) > MAX_BYTES) s.files.shift();
30:   if (s.pending && !s.files.some(file => file.id === s.pending!.id)) s.pending = null;
31:   schedule(s); emit(s); return id;
32: }
33: export function removeSessionFile(id: string) { const s=state(); s.files=s.files.filter(file=>file.id!==id); if(s.pending?.id===id)s.pending=null; schedule(s); emit(s); }
34: export function clearSessionFiles() { const s=state(); s.files=[]; s.pending=null; schedule(s); emit(s); }
35: export function handoffSessionFile(id: string, path: string): boolean { prune(); const s=state(); if(!s.files.some(file=>file.id===id))return false; s.pending={id,path}; return true; }
36: export function takeSessionFile(path: string): File | null {
37:   prune(); const s=state(); if(!s.pending || s.pending.path!==path)return null;
38:   const item=s.files.find(file=>file.id===s.pending!.id); s.pending=null;
39:   return item ? new File([item.blob], item.name, {type:item.blob.type}) : null;
40: }

Actually a cleaner and more reliable approach for document scan: detect the document via a combination of edge detection + Hough lines to find 4 dominant lines. But that's a lot.

Given constraints, let me implement a solid, testable detector:

- Non-maximum suppression? no, keep it simpler
- Threshold: adaptive using mean + k*std of gradient magnitude
- Binary image, then find largest connected component of edge pixels? Edges are thin; better to fill.

Alternative simpler robust approach: **corner-based quad detection via extreme points**. Compute binary edge map, then among edge pixels find the four extremes along rotated axes (0°/45°/90°/135°) — i.e., for each of 4 directions, the pixel maximizing projection. Then order them. That yields a quad. It works reasonably when the document is the dominant edge source. And it's cheap.

Hmm, but with a cluttered background it fails. Add confidence check: quad area must be > 25% of frame, convex, and edges must be reasonably straight.

Actually let me do something more principled and still tractable:

1. Downscale to max 480 wide.
2. Grayscale.
3. Compute gradient magnitude (Sobel).
4. Threshold: top ~10% gradient pixels → edge mask.
5. Find connected components; take the largest by bounding-box area that is "line-like" (sparse)? No — document edges form a sparse connected rectangle. The largest connected component of the edge mask should be the document outline if it's closed. Good.
6. Trace that component's convex hull.
7. Approximate hull with 4 lines using... hmm.

Simpler: after getting the largest component's pixel set, compute the convex hull, then find the minimum-area quadrilateral? Or: use the hull and pick the 4 points that maximize area among hull points (rotating calipers-ish). Actually a common trick: take the hull, then approximate with Douglas–Peucker tolerance until 4 points.

I'll do: largest component → convex hull → RDP with increasing epsilon until ≤4 points; if exactly 4 (or 5 with near-collinear merge) → candidate quad. Then validate: area ratio > 0.15, corners spread, convex.

That's implementable in ~200 lines. Then perspective transform: compute homography from quad → target rect, warp with canvas per-pixel (or use CSS-free manual sampling). For quality, warp at output resolution = estimated document size (based on quad edge lengths), capped.

Let me implement homography solve (8x8 linear system) and inverse mapping with bilinear sampling. That's standard.

Better robust-ish approach without heavy libs:

- Threshold at mean + k*std.

- If validation fails, fall back to a margin inset (e.g., 5% inset) quad = full frame.

Thinking
Now build the scanner engine: document edge detection. Key modules:

1. `src/engines/document-detect.ts` — pure functions: grayscale, blur, Sobel gradient, find quadrilateral corners. Must be testable in Node (unit tests with .mjs using --experimental-strip-types).

2. `src/engines/perspective.ts` — compute homography from 4 points, warp to rectangle using canvas 2D (or manual bilinear sampling). For a static site with canvas, I can compute a projective transform and use ctx.setTransform? Canvas doesn't do projective natively, so manual warping with bilinear sampling is the reliable way. But that's O(w*h) in JS — for 1600x2200 that's 3.5M pixels, fine (~100-300ms).

Actually, a common trick: split the quad into two triangles and use affine transforms per triangle with clipping. Faster and hardware-accelerated. But seams possible. Manual bilinear sampling is more accurate and simpler to reason about. Let me do manual sampling with an inverse homography — accurate and correct.

Let me design the algorithm properly:

**Edge detection (document-detect.ts):**
- Downscale to max 480px for detection speed.
- Grayscale.
- Gaussian blur (3x3 or separable 5-tap).
- Sobel gradient magnitude.
- Find the largest quadrilateral: a robust simple approach is:
  - Use gradient magnitude image.
  - Compute bounding approach: find the strongest contour? Without OpenCV that's work.

A pragmatic, well-tested approach used in many JS scanners:
1. Threshold: compute mean gradient; pick pixels above threshold.
2. Find extreme points: for each of 4 directions (top-left, top-right, bottom-right, bottom-left), maximize/minimize (x+y) and (x-y) among strong edge pixels. This gives 4 corner candidates.
3. Validate: the quad should be convex, have area > 20% of frame, and corners should be reasonably separated.

This is simple, deterministic, and works well for document photos on a contrasting background. But it can be fooled by strong edges in the background. Mitigate by:
- Restricting to pixels near the border of a plausible region? 
- Better: use a "largest bright region" approach, since documents are usually brighter than the background: threshold on luminance (Otsu), take largest connected component, find its corners via the extreme-point method. That's quite robust and fast.

Combining both signals gives better results. Let's implement:
- Otsu threshold on blurred grayscale at low res.
- Consider both polarities (bright document on dark bg, dark on bright).
- For each polarity, find largest connected component (flood fill / union-find, iterative stack).
- Compute area, and corner extremes of that component.
- Score = component area; require >= 25% of image.
- Pick the best polarity.
- Fallback: if no component passes, use gradient-based extreme points; if that fails, return null → UI uses full frame.

Let me be careful about "the results are correct" requirement. Also user asked "dari kamera otomatis mendeteksi dokumennya jika di arahkan" — auto detection when pointed. So live detection during camera preview: run detection every ~600ms on downscaled frames drawn to an offscreen canvas, draw a quad overlay on a canvas on top of video. Then on capture, use the last detected quad (or full frame).

Also manual corner adjustment: draggable handles.

Output: JPG (with perspective-corrected crop) or PDF (via pdf-lib, JPEG embedded). Also filters like "scan look" (grayscale/contrast boost) — Google Drive scanner does color/grayscale/black-and-white modes. Let's add color, grayscale, high-contrast B&W. That matches "hasil scan nya sama seperti hasil scan di fitur scanner google drive".

Multi-page: Google Drive scan supports multiple pages → single PDF. That's a natural extension and genuinely useful. Keep scope reasonable but correct: support capturing multiple pages and exporting one PDF. I'll implement it — it's the core of a document scanner.

Let me write:

**document-detect.ts** (pure, testable):
```ts
export type Point = { x: number; y: number };
export type Quad = [Point, Point, Point, Point]; // tl, tr, br, bl in normalized 0..1 coords

export function otsuThreshold(gray: Uint8ClampedArray): number
export function detectDocumentCorners(gray: Uint8ClampedArray, width: number, height: number): Quad | null
export function orderCorners(points: Point[]): Quad
export function quadArea(quad: Quad): number
export function isConvex(quad: Quad): boolean
```

Then perspective:
```ts
export function homography(src: Point[], dst: Point[]): number[] | null  // 3x3
export function warpPerspective(source: ImageData, quad: Quad, outWidth, outHeight): ImageData
```

Let me implement homography solving properly. Mapping from destination rect to source quad is what we need (inverse mapping for sampling). Solve 8 unknowns with linear system (Gaussian elimination).

Standard: for each correspondence (x,y) -> (u,v):
u = (h0 x + h1 y + h2) / (h6 x + h7 y + 1)
v = (h3 x + h4 y + h5) / (h6 x + h7 y + 1)

8 equations. Solve with Gaussian elimination.

Then for each destination pixel (u,v) in rect [0,w]x[0,h], we map to source using inverse mapping: Actually we want source->dest? We iterate over destination pixels and sample source. So we need mapping dest->src, i.e. set up correspondences dest rect corners -> source quad corners. That gives H mapping dest to source directly. 

So: src points = dest rect corners, dst points = quad corners. Solve H: destRect -> quad. Then for each dest pixel, compute (x', y') = H(x,y), sample bilinear from source. Output.

That's exactly right.

Sampling must clamp and handle out-of-bounds → white.

Performance: for 2x3M pixels, ~3.5M iterations with Math ops. In JS maybe 200-500ms. Acceptable. Cap output dimension at e.g. 2400px on the long side for PDF sanity, and keep aspect ratio of detected quad.

Output size: Google Drive scan produces a rectified doc with aspect ratio from quad. Compute width/height from quad's average edge lengths, scale so long side <= 2200 (approx A4 at 200dpi ~1654x2339). Let's cap long edge at 2400 px.

4: async function collectHtml(directory) {
5:   const files = [];
6:   for (const entry of await readdir(directory, { withFileTypes: true })) {
7:     const path = join(directory, entry.name);
8:     if (entry.isDirectory()) files.push(...await collectHtml(path));
9:     else if (entry.name.endsWith('.html')) files.push(path);
10:   }
11:   return files;
12: }

15: const scriptHashes = new Set();
16: 
17: for (const file of htmlFiles) {
18:   const html = await readFile(file, 'utf8');
19:   const meta = html.match(/<meta[^>]*http-equiv="content-security-policy"[^>]*content="([^"]+)"/i);
20:   if (!meta) continue;
21:   const scriptSrc = meta[1].split(';').map(part => part.trim()).find(part => part.startsWith('script-src'));
22:   for (const match of scriptSrc?.matchAll(/'sha(?:256|384|512)-[^']+'/g) ?? []) scriptHashes.add(match[0]);
23: }

32:   if (!line.trim().startsWith('Content-Security-Policy:')) {
33:     output.push(line);
34:     continue;
35:   }
36:   const policy = line.slice(line.indexOf(':') + 1).trim();
37:   const directives = policy.split(';').map(part => part.trim()).filter(Boolean);
38:   const merged = directives.map(directive => {
39:     if (!directive.startsWith('script-src')) return directive;
40:     const sources = directive.split(/\s+/).slice(1).filter(source => !source.startsWith("'sha"));
41:     return ['script-src', ...sources, ...scriptHashes].join(' ');
42:   });
43:   output.push(`  Content-Security-Policy: ${merged.join('; ')}`);
44: }
45:

Now let's write the scanner engine: edge detection. I need real logic:

1. Capture frame from camera (video element) or file input.
2. Downscale to e.g. 320px wide grayscale.
3. Detect document quad: approach — use a simple approach: compute edge magnitude (Sobel), then find document boundary by scanning for largest bright quadrilateral? A robust simple approach: 
   - Convert to grayscale.
   - Find the largest connected region of "paper-like" pixels? Fragile.
   - Alternative pragmatic approach used by many web scanners: use `jsfeat` or OpenCV.js — too heavy.

Let me implement a self-contained quad detector:
- Downscale to width 256.
- Grayscale + blur.
- Sobel gradient magnitude.
- For each of 4 sides, find the most prominent line using a Hough-like accumulation? Simpler: 
- Use a "contour tracing" approach: threshold gradient, then find the largest connected component, then compute its convex hull, then approximate a quadrilateral by finding 4 extreme points (topmost, rightmost, bottommost, leftmost) — works reasonably for a document sheet photographed against a contrasting background.

Actually better and quite reliable: after thresholding the gradient magnitude and cleaning, take the largest connected component's convex hull, then find the 4 corners by maximizing |x+y|, |x-y| minima/maxima (standard trick): 
- top-left = min(x+y), bottom-right = max(x+y), top-right = max(x-y), bottom-left = min(x-y).
That's the classic "extreme corner" heuristic and works well for quadrilaterals.

To make gradient blob include the whole sheet, better to threshold on "paper vs background" — but contrast may vary. Combining edge detection + filling: I'll do gradient threshold, dilate, then find largest component, and use its bounding-quad. That gives the paper outline if borders are the strongest edges.

Fallback: if the detected quad area is < 20% of the frame, return null → use full frame.

Then perspective transform: compute homography from the 4 corners to a target rectangle. Output size: choose based on max width/height of quad edges, capped (e.g. max 2600 px long side).

Edge detection details:

```ts
export type Quad = [{x,y},{x,y},{x,y},{x,y}];

export function detectDocumentQuad(data: Uint8ClampedArray, width: number, height: number): Quad | null
```
Steps:
1. gray array Float32Array.
2. box blur 3x3 (or integral image approach).
3. Sobel magnitude.
4. threshold: use mean + 1.5*std, clamp min.
5. Binary mask; dilate 1.
6. Connected components (BFS with stack, 4-connectivity) → largest area.
7. Collect points of largest component; compute extreme corners.
8. Validate: quad area vs image area >= 0.15, and each corner distance from ideal >= ... Also check convexity and that the component touches margins? Not strictly.
9. Sort corners into TL, TR, BR, BL consistently.

Extreme-corner method returns 4 points; assign by geometry:
- TL: min(x+y); BR: max(x+y); TR: max(x-y); BL: min(x-y).
Then ensure distinct.

Order for warp: TL, TR, BR, BL.

Then compute output size: width = avg(len(TL-TR), len(BL-BR)); height = avg(len(TL-BL), len(TR-BR)). Cap long side 2600.

I'll implement warp with inverse mapping and bilinear interpolation. For a 2600px output that's ~ up to 5M samples in JS — acceptable (a few hundred ms).

Enhance: compute per-channel histogram percentiles (2% and 98%) then linear stretch, plus gamma? Keep white balance = per-channel stretch, then luminance contrast stretch. Simple and correct.

Thinking
Detection fails on real page. Let me debug. The threshold: mean + 1.1*deviation. On a synthetic image with a hard edge, magnitude is concentrated on the edge; mean is small, deviation maybe bigger. Threshold = max(12, mean+1.1*dev). Sobel magnitude on a 210-step edge: gx = 4*210 = 840. So max magnitude ~840, mean maybe ~ (edge pixels share) small. dev is large. mean+1.1dev could be large — e.g. if p = fraction of edge pixels, mean ≈ 840p, second moment ≈ 840²p, var = 840²p - (840p)², dev ≈ 840√(p - p²) ≈ 840√p for small p. So threshold ≈ 840p + 1.1*840√p. For p = 0.01, that's 8.4 + 1.1*84 = 101. Edge magnitude 840 > 101 → passes. Hmm should work.

Wait—the perimeter of the box is roughly 2*(420+620)=2080 pixels, out of 700*800=560,000 → p ≈ 0.0037 but blurred edges expand to ~3px wide → ~6000 px → p ≈ 0.011. So threshold ~ 840*0.011 + 1.1*840*0.105 ≈ 9.2+97 = 106. Edge is 840 > 106. Fine.

So mask should be the box outline. Then dilation joins it into one component with ~6000+ px. minimumPixels = width*height*0.08 = 560000*0.08 = 44,800. The outline component is only ~6000 pixels! That's why it fails — I required the component to cover 8% of the frame, but the component is the *outline*, not the filled region.

That's the bug. I should not require the outline component to be 8%; rather require the quad area to be ≥12% of the frame. Let me remove the component-size test or lower it considerably, and rely on area + edge length checks.

Let me fix: minimum component pixels = max(64, width*height*0.002) — just to reject specks. And keep area threshold.

Also for the "tiny page" test: quad of 25x25=625 px area out of 160,000 = 0.39% < 12% → rejected. Good.

Let me also double check orderQuad works with extreme points of a filled outline. For an outline component, the extreme points: min sum = top-left corner, max sum = bottom-right, max diff = top-right, min diff = bottom-left. Good.

Let me update the code.

Deteksi gagal karena kesalahan saya: komponen terhubung adalah garis tepi (~6000 px), bukan bidang penuh, jadi syarat 8% area mustahil. Saya perbaiki.

 95	  ];

102	
103	  // Three of the four corners sit on the dark desk, so a correct warp must
104	  // sample almost only paper. A wrong homography would drag the desk inside.
105	  const luminanceAt = (x, y) => {
106	    const index = (y * warped.width + x) * 4;
107	    return (warped.data[index] + warped.data[index + 1] + warped.data[index + 2]) / 3;
108	  };
109	  const inset = 6;
110	  const samples = [
111	    luminanceAt(inset, inset),

317:       if (aliveRef.current) setBusy(false);
318:     }
319:   }
320: 
321:   async function buildPdf(image: ImageData): Promise<Blob> {
322:     if (image.width < 2 || image.height < 2) throw new Error('empty');
323:     const { PDFDocument } = await import('pdf-lib');
324:     const document = await PDFDocument.create();
325:     const png = imageDataToCanvas(image);
326:     const encoded = await canvasToBlob(png, 'image/png', 1);
327:     const embedded = await document.embedPng(await encoded.arrayBuffer());
328:     // A4 in points, then fit the page inside it with a 10 mm margin.
329:     const pageWidth = 595.28;
330:     const pageHeight = 841.89;
331:     const margin = (10 * 72) / 25.4;
332:     const scale = Math.min((pageWidth - margin * 2) / image.width, (pageHeight - margin * 2) / image.height);
333:     const width = image.width * scale;
334:     const height = image.height * scale;
335:     const page = document.addPage([width + margin * 2, height + margin * 2]);
336:     page.drawImage(embedded, { x: (page.getWidth() - width) / 2, y: (page.getHeight() - height) / 2, width, height });
337:     const bytes = await document.save();
338:     return new Blob([bytes as BlobPart], { type: 'application/pdf' });
339:   }
340: 
341:   function reset() {
342:     stopCamera();
343:     releaseOutputs();
344:     sourceRef.current = null;
345:     setCorners(null);
346:     setFrame(null);
347:     setDetected(false);
348:     setPreviewUrl('');
349:     setNotice('');
350:     setError('');
351:     setStage('idle');
352:   }

356:     ? { left: `${(point.x / frame.width) * 100}%`, top: `${(point.y / frame.height) * 100}%` }
357:     : undefined;

# Doculven

Doculven is a bilingual static site with 10 image and document tools processed in the browser. It has one Indonesian and one English route per tool, with relevant formats and size presets grouped inside shared workspaces. See [IMPLEMENTATION_STATUS.md](IMPLEMENTATION_STATUS.md) and [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) for current scope, verification, and hosting.

## Development

Requires Node.js 22.13 or newer and pnpm.

```sh
pnpm install
pnpm dev
pnpm lint
pnpm check
pnpm test:unit
pnpm build
pnpm performance:landing
pnpm preview
pnpm test:e2e
```

`pnpm performance:landing` runs after `pnpm build` and totals the gzip size of the JavaScript referenced by the Indonesian landing page, enforcing the 100,000-byte budget. It is an asset budget check, not a Lighthouse or field Core Web Vitals audit.

The build's `prebuild` step regenerates the LGPL source archive for the locked `heic-to` decoder at `public/licenses/heic-to-1.6.7-source.zip`. `pnpm test:unit` checks that archive against the installed package and checks the source privacy boundaries and static-host header policy. See [docs/DEPENDENCY_LICENSES.md](docs/DEPENDENCY_LICENSES.md) for the package inventory and redistribution conditions.

`pnpm assets:prepare` re-encodes the checked-in synthetic landing sources into local WebP assets using the installed Playwright Chromium browser. The public site self-hosts Plus Jakarta Sans and JetBrains Mono; font and image provenance is recorded in [ASSET_CREDITS.md](ASSET_CREDITS.md).

Install the Playwright browsers before running browser tests: `pnpm exec playwright install`. The full local-model integration run is `pnpm exec playwright test tests/background.spec.ts --workers=1` and takes longer than the other suites.

## Static deployment

Netlify is the deployment target. The site is static; nothing here needs a server runtime.

`netlify.toml` holds the build command (`pnpm build`), the `dist` publish directory, the `/` to `/id/` redirect, and long-lived caching for hashed assets. Netlify also applies `dist/_headers`, so the CSP, framing, and referrer policies are enforced as real response headers.

Set `PUBLIC_SITE_URL` in the Netlify environment to the final HTTPS origin, for example `https://doculven.netlify.app` or a custom domain. Without it, canonical and sitemap URLs fall back to `https://doculven.example`, which `scripts/verify-pages-output.mjs` rejects on purpose.

The CSP is hash-based. `pnpm build` emits per-page hashes through Astro's `security.csp`, then `postbuild` merges them into `dist/_headers` with `scripts/generate-hea
